How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n     4→\n     5→i. The dataset is distributed as one NWB file per session under `data/sub-<subject_id>/`. All sessions are found with a single glob over that layout, and each file is opened with `pynwb` and processed once. Subjects, trials, and units are then read from within each file (`nwb.subject`, `nwb.trials`, `nwb.units`, `nwb.acquisition`).\n     6→\n     7→ii. Finding all data:\n     8→```python\n     9→files = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, 'sub-*', '*.nwb')))\n    10→...\n    11→for i, path in enumerate(files, 1):\n    12→    res = process_session(path)\n    13→```\n    14→\n    15→Loading one session:\n    16→```python\n    17→with NWBHDF5IO(path, mode='r', load_namespaces=True) as io:\n    18→    nwb = io.read()\n    19→    units = nwb.units\n    20→    trials = nwb.trials.to_dataframe()\n    21→    bev = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralEvents'].time_series\n    22→```\n    23→\n    24→iii. NWB is the published format for this dataset and `pynwb` is its standard reader. Since there is one file per session, the directory listing is the complete set of sessions and a glob is sufficient; sorting it makes the session order deterministic. The resulting counts (174 files, 28 subjects) match `assetsSummary` in `data/dandiset.yaml`.\n    25→\n    26→## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n    27→\n    28→i. Each NWB file records its animal in `nwb.subject.subject_id`, a numeric string such as `'440956'`. That value is read for every session and carried through to assembly, where `subjects` is the sorted set of unique ids and `subject_idx` gives each session's index into that list.\n    29→\n    30→ii. Per session:\n    31→```python\n    32→return {\n    33→    'session_id': nwb.identifier,\n    34→    'subject': nwb.subject.subject_id,\n    35→    ...\n    36→```\n    37→\n    38→At assembly:\n    39→```python\n    40→subjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\n    41→sub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n    42→...\n    43→'subjects': subjects,\n    44→'subject_idx': np.array([sub_ix[r['subject']] for r in results], np.int32),\n    45→```\n    46→\n    47→iii. `subject_id` is the canonical animal identifier in the file, and the containing folder name (`sub-440956`) is derived from it, so no separate grouping step is needed. This gives 28 subjects with 3-10 sessions each, matching the dandiset. The numeric id differs from the mouse name used in the papers — `nwb.identifier` is e.g. `SC015_20190207_120657_s1`, so subject `440956` is mouse `SC015` — but the numeric id is what the NWB subject field provides, so it is used directly.\n    48→\n    49→## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n    50→\n    51→i. One NWB file is one session, so no grouping or splitting is needed. Each session is identified by `nwb.identifier` (e.g. `SC015_20190207_120657_s1`, encoding mouse, date, time and session number), recorded per session and also listed in `metadata['session_info']`. Session order in the output follows the sorted file list.\n    52→\n    53→ii.\n    54→```python\n    55→files = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, 'sub-*', '*.nwb')))\n    56→```\n    57→\n    58→```python\n    59→'session_id': nwb.identifier,\n    60→```\n    61→\n    62→```python\n    63→'session_info': [\n    64→    {'session_id': r['session_id'], 'subject': r['subject'],\n    65→     'n_trials': r['n_trials'], 'n_units': r['n_units']}\n    66→    for r in results\n    67→],\n    68→```\n    69→\n    70→iii. The dandiset already stores one session per file, so the file boundary is the session boundary and nothing has to be inferred. Because the filename embeds the acquisition timestamp (`sub-440956_ses-20190207T120657_...`), sorting the paths puts sessions in chronological order within each subject. 173 of the 174 files reach the output; the one exception is dropped for having no quality-controlled units (see 2-c).\n    71→\n    72→## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n    73→\n    74→i. Trials come from the NWB trials table (`nwb.trials`), one row per behavioural trial, with exactly one go-cue event per row. The row count is checked against the number of go-cue events.\n    75→\n    76→ii.\n    77→```python\n    78→trials = nwb.trials.to_dataframe()\n    79→go = np.asarray(bev['go_start_times'].timestamps)\n    80→assert len(go) == len(trials), f'{len(go)} go cues vs {len(trials)} trials'\n    81→```\n    82→\n    83→iii. Trials are clearly defined by the trials table, so it is used directly rather than re-deriving boundaries from the event streams. `go_start_times` has exactly one event per trial in all 174 sessions, which makes the mapping unambiguous. This is not true of the other trial-phase events: `sample_start_times` and `delay_start_times` can have several entries per trial, because a lick during the sample or delay replays that epoch.\n    84→\n    85→## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n    86→\n    87→i. Trials are filtered only for absence of spike data, in two cases. First, trials outside `units/obs_intervals`: in 8 sessions the ephys recording starts after the behaviour, leaving up to 376 leading trials with no spikes. Second, `free_water` trials, which have no spikes anywhere in the window even though `obs_intervals` covers them. A session is dropped entirely if fewer than 2 trials survive. No behavioural quality filter is applied.\n    88→\n    89→ii.\n    90→```python\n    91→# filter behavioral trials with no spike data\n    92→oi_off = np.asarray(units['obs_intervals'].data)\n    93→oi_start = np.concatenate([[0], oi_off[:-1]])\n    94→obs = np.asarray(units['obs_intervals'].target.data)[oi_start[good[0]]:oi_off[good[0]]]\n    95→keep = np.isin(np.round(trials['start_time'].values, 4), np.round(obs[:, 0], 4))\n    96→assert keep.sum() == len(obs), f'{keep.sum()} matched vs {len(obs)} observed'\n    97→\n    98→# filter no water trials\n    99→keep &= trials['free_water'].values == 0\n   100→if keep.sum() < 2:\n   101→    return None\n   102→trials = trials[keep]\n   103→go = go[keep]\n   104→```\n   105→\n   106→iii. `obs_intervals` is the file's own record of which trials were observed, and its start times match `trials.start_time`, which makes the mapping exact. The `free_water` exclusion was found empirically — those trials are covered by `obs_intervals` yet contain no spikes at all — and accounts for 2,449 of the 2,451 all-zero trials remaining after the `obs_intervals` filter.\n   107→\n   108→Early-lick and no-response (`ignore`) trials are deliberately kept, even though the data paper states \"Early lick trials and no response trials were excluded for analysis\", because both are required decoder outputs here. The 2-trial minimum is the target format's requirement.\n   109→\n   110→Together these remove 3,510 of the 94,370 trials in the retained sessions (3.7%), leaving 90,860.\n   111→\n   112→## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n   113→\n   114→i. Neural data is derived from `units/spike_times`, the sorted spike times of each unit in session-absolute seconds. Only units with `classification == 'good'` contribute (see 2-c). The go-cue times (`BehavioralEvents/go_start_times`) are the other input, used to place the bin edges.\n   115→\n   116→ii.\n   117→```python\n   118→offs = np.asarray(units['spike_times'].data)\n   119→allst = np.asarray(units['spike_times'].target.data)\n   120→starts = np.concatenate([[0], offs[:-1]])\n   121→\n   122→edges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n   123→rates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\n   124→for r, u in enumerate(good):\n   125→    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n   126→```\n   127→\n   128→iii. `spike_times` is the only neural representation in the file, so firing rates are computed from it directly.\n   129→\n   130→## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n   131→\n   132→i. Spike times are converted to per-bin firing rates in Hz. For each good unit, the bin edges for every trial are built as one flat array of absolute times, `np.searchsorted` gives the running spike count at each edge, and differencing adjacent counts gives the spike count per bin. Counts are divided by the bin width to give Hz. No smoothing, normalisation, or baseline subtraction is applied.\n   133→\n   134→ii.\n   135→```python\n   136→edges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n   137→rates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\n   138→for r, u in enumerate(good):\n   139→    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n   140→    # running spike total at each edge; differencing gives the count per bin\n   141→    pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n   142→    rates[r] = np.diff(pos, axis=1)\n   143→rates /= BIN                                  # counts -> Hz\n   144→```\n   145→\n   146→iii. Firing rate is computed from the binned spike count over the 50 ms window defined in the instructions. This also matches the reference code, which calls `sliding_histogram(..., rate=True)` and returns `binSpikes / bin_width`.\n   147→\n   148→## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n   149→\n   150→i. Only units with `units/classification == 'good'` are kept. No thresholds are applied to any individual quality metric. A session with no such units is dropped entirely. This retains 69,453 of 272,227 units (25.5%), a median of 390 per session.\n   151→\n   152→ii.\n   153→```python\n   154→# ---- units: QC classifier verdict ----------------------------------\n   155→cls = _text(units['classification'])\n   156→good = np.flatnonzero(cls == 'good')\n   157→if good.size == 0:\n   158→    return None\n   159→```\n   160→\n   161→iii. `classification` is the verdict of the spike-sorting quality-control classifier described in `ChenLiuEtAl2023_SpikeSortingQC.pdf`.\n   162→\n   163→`units/unit_quality` ('good' / 'multi') is deliberately not used: it is an older label that disagrees on 12.2% of the classifier-good units and is far more permissive.\n   164→\n   165→The one dropped session (`sub-440958_ses-20190216T162508`) has `classification` and `anno_name` set to NaN for all 1,852 units, i.e. it was never quality-controlled. Excluding it gives 173 sessions and 69,453 good units, against the white paper's 173 sessions and 69,943 units.\n   166→\n   167→## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n   168→\n   169→i. Spike times and event times are already on the same session-absolute clock, so no alignment step is needed. The bin edges relative to the go cue are added to each trial's go-cue time to give the absolute time window for that trial, and the spikes are binned against those edges directly.\n   170→\n   171→ii.\n   172→```python\n   173→go = np.asarray(bev['go_start_times'].timestamps)\n   174→```\n   175→\n   176→```python\n   177→edges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n   178→rates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\n   179→for r, u in enumerate(good):\n   180→    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n   181→    pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n   182→    rates[r] = np.diff(pos, axis=1)\n   183→```\n   184→\n   185→iii. Everything in the NWB file is timestamped on one global clock, so aligning to the go cue only requires looking up each trial's go-cue time and taking the window around it. There is no resampling or interpolation, and no per-stream offset to correct.\n   186→\n   187→## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n   188→\n   189→i. Spike times are binned into 80 non-overlapping 50 ms bins spanning -2.5 s to +1.5 s relative to the go cue. The bin grid is defined once, as 81 edges relative to the go cue, and reused for every trial and session, so every trial has the same 80 timepoints.\n   190→\n   191→ii.\n   192→```python\n   193→# trial window relative to the go cue, and bin width, in seconds\n   194→T_START, T_STOP = -2.5, 1.5\n   195→BIN = 0.05\n   196→N_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))             \n   197→\n   198→REL_EDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)         \n   199→CENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        \n   200→```\n   201→\n   202→```python\n   203→edges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n   204→```\n   205→\n   206→iii. The window and the 50 ms bin width are set by the instructions. Defining the grid once as offsets from the go cue gives a constant 80 timepoints per trial, which the target format requires.\n   207→\n   208→## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_from_tone_onset* derived from?\n   209→\n   210→i. From `sample_start_times`, the tone onsets of the session, together with the go cue of each trial. The tone taken for a trial is the **last one** before its go cue.\n   211→\n   212→ii. The tone of each trial:\n   213→```python\n   214→sample = np.asarray(bev['sample_start_times'].timestamps)\n   215→tone = sample[np.searchsorted(sample, go, side='left') - 1]\n   216→```\n   217→\n   218→iii. An early lick replays the sample epoch, so a trial can carry more than one tone; the last one before the go cue is the one the animal actually used.\n   219→\n   220→## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_from_tone_onset*?\n   221→\n   222→i. The bins sit around the go cue, so the value of a bin is its center plus the gap between the tone and the go cue.\n   223→\n   224→ii. The bin center, and the shift that turns them into time from the tone:\n   225→```python\n   226→CENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        \n   227→```\n   228→```python\n   229→# bins sit around the go cue, values are seconds since the tone\n   230→time_from_tone = CENTERS[None, :] + (go - tone)[:, None]\n   231→```\n   232→\n   233→iii. No additional processing needed other than find the tone onset time.\n   234→\n   235→## 3-c. How is `input` *time_from_tone_onset* aligned with the neural data?\n   236→\n   237→i. It is how the neural binnning grid is defined.\n   238→\n   239→ii. The grid, defined once as offsets from the go cue, and laid on each trial's go cue to bin the spikes:\n   240→```python\n   241→T_START, T_STOP = -2.5, 1.5\n   242→BIN = 0.05\n   243→N_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))             \n   244→\n   245→REL_EDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)         \n   246→CENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        \n   247→```\n   248→```python\n   249→edges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n   250→```\n   251→\n   252→iii. N/A\n   253→\n   254→## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *photostim* derived from?\n   255→\n   256→i. From `photostim_onset` and `photostim_duration` in the trials table, with `start_time` and the go cue used to place them on the trial's time axis.\n   257→\n   258→ii. The onset and offset of the stimulation, relative to the go cue:\n   259→```python\n   260→on_s = trials['photostim_onset'].values\n   261→has = on_s != 'N/A'\n   262→stim_on[has] = trials['start_time'].values[has] + on_s[has].astype(float) - go[has]\n   263→stim_off[has] = stim_on[has] + trials['photostim_duration'].values[has].astype(float)\n   264→```\n   265→\n   266→iii. The onsets are stored as strings measured from trial start, with `'N/A'` on the trials that were not stimulated, so they have to be converted and re-expressed against the go cue the bins are aligned on.\n   267→\n   268→## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *photostim*?\n   269→\n   270→i. A bin is 1 where its center falls between the onset and the offset of the stimulation and 0 elsewhere, so the input is a binary time series rather than a per trial flag.\n   271→\n   272→ii. The bins the light is on for:\n   273→```python\n   274→# NaN compares False, so non-stim trials stay 0\n   275→photostim = ((CENTERS[None, :] >= stim_on[:, None])\n   276→             & (CENTERS[None, :] < stim_off[:, None]))\n   277→```\n   278→\n   279→iii. A trial without stimulation keeps NaN bounds, and NaN compares false, so all of its bins come out 0 without a separate branch.\n   280→\n   281→## 4-c. How is `input` *photostim* aligned with the neural data?\n   282→\n   283→i. The onset and offset are expressed relative to the go cue, which is the event the neural bins are aligned on, so they can be compared against the bin center directly.\n   284→\n   285→ii. The onset put on the same axis as the bins:\n   286→```python\n   287→stim_on[has] = trials['start_time'].values[has] + on_s[has].astype(float) - go[has]\n   288→```\n   289→\n   290→iii. N/A\n   291→\n   292→## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *choice* derived from?\n   293→\n   294→i. There is no choice column in the file. Choice is derived from two trials-table columns: `trial_instruction` (`'left'` / `'right'`, the side the tone instructed) and `outcome` (`'hit'` / `'miss'` / `'ignore'`). A hit means the animal licked the instructed side, a miss means it licked the other side, and an `ignore` means it never licked.\n   295→\n   296→ii.\n   297→```python\n   298→SIDE_CODE = {'left': 0, 'right': 1}\n   299→CHOICE_NO_LICK = 2               # no lick in the response window\n   300→```\n   301→\n   302→```python\n   303→# choice is not stored; derive it from instruction x outcome\n   304→outcome_s = trials['outcome'].values\n   305→side = np.array([SIDE_CODE[x] for x in trials['trial_instruction'].values])\n   306→choice = np.where(outcome_s == 'ignore', CHOICE_NO_LICK,\n   307→                  np.where(outcome_s == 'hit', side, 1 - side))\n   308→```\n   309→\n   310→iii. The animal's actual lick direction is not stored, but it is fully determined by the instructed side and the outcome, so it is derived from those two columns. `outcome == 'ignore'` was verified to mean no lick anywhere in `[go, go + 1.5]`, with no exceptions, so choice is genuinely undefined on those trials and gets its own third value.\n   311→\n   312→## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *choice*?\n   313→\n   314→i. The derived choice is coded as `0` left, `1` right, `2` no lick, and written into row 0 of the per-trial output array, repeated across all 80 bins. `output_values[0]` names the three codes.\n   315→\n   316→ii.\n   317→```python\n   318→SIDE_CODE = {'left': 0, 'right': 1}\n   319→CHOICE_NO_LICK = 2               # no lick in the response window\n   320→\n   321→OUTPUT_VALUES = [\n   322→    ['left', 'right', 'no lick'],\n   323→    ...\n   324→]\n   325→```\n   326→\n   327→```python\n   328→# choice is not stored; derive it from instruction x outcome\n   329→outcome_s = trials['outcome'].values\n   330→side = np.array([SIDE_CODE[x] for x in trials['trial_instruction'].values])\n   331→choice = np.where(outcome_s == 'ignore', CHOICE_NO_LICK,\n   332→                  np.where(outcome_s == 'hit', side, 1 - side))\n   333→\n   334→# per-trial values are repeated across bins so all outputs share one array\n   335→out = np.empty((n_trials, len(OUTPUT_NAMES), N_BINS), np.int8)\n   336→out[:, 0, :] = choice[:, None]\n   337→```\n   338→\n   339→iii. `left = 0` and `right = 1` follow the instructions, and a third class is defined for the no-lick case. Choice is one value per trial, so it is repeated across the 80 bins to keep all four outputs in a single `(n_output, n_timepoints)` array.\n   340→\n   341→## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *outcome* derived from?\n   342→\n   343→i. Outcome comes directly from the `outcome` column of the trials table, which already holds the strings `'ignore'`, `'miss'`, and `'hit'`.\n   344→\n   345→ii.\n   346→```python\n   347→outcome_s = trials['outcome'].values\n   348→```\n   349→\n   350→iii. The trials table stores the outcome explicitly with exactly the three categories the instructions ask for, so no derivation is needed.\n   351→\n   352→## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *outcome*?\n   353→\n   354→i. The three strings are mapped to `0` ignore, `1` miss, `2` hit via a fixed dictionary, and written into row 1 of the output array, repeated across all 80 bins.\n   355→\n   356→ii.\n   357→```python\n   358→OUTCOME_CODE = {'ignore': 0, 'miss': 1, 'hit': 2}\n   359→```\n   360→\n   361→```python\n   362→out[:, 1, :] = np.array([OUTCOME_CODE[x] for x in outcome_s])[:, None]\n   363→```\n   364→\n   365→iii. The code assignment follows the instructions. Outcome is one value per trial, so it is repeated across bins like the other per-trial outputs.\n   366→\n   367→## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *early_lick* derived from?\n   368→\n   369→i. From the `early_lick` column of the trials table, which holds the strings `'no early'` and `'early'`.\n   370→\n   371→ii.\n   372→```python\n   373→trials['early_lick'].values\n   374→```\n   375→\n   376→iii. The trials table flags early licking explicitly, so no derivation is needed. The lick that sets the flag occurs during the sample or delay epoch, before the go cue, so the event itself falls inside the -2.5 s window even though the flag is stored per trial.\n   377→\n   378→## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *early_lick*?\n   379→\n   380→i. The two strings are mapped to `0` no, `1` yes via a fixed dictionary, and written into row 2 of the output array, repeated across all 80 bins.\n   381→\n   382→ii.\n   383→```python\n   384→EARLY_CODE = {'no early': 0, 'early': 1}\n   385→```\n   386→\n   387→```python\n   388→out[:, 2, :] = np.array([EARLY_CODE[x] for x in trials['early_lick'].values])[:, None]\n   389→```\n   390→\n   391→iii. The code assignment follows the instructions. One value per trial, so repeated across bins like the other per-trial outputs.\n   392→\n   393→## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *tongue_y_position* derived from?\n   394→\n   395→i. From `acquisition/BehavioralTimeSeries/Camera0_side_TongueTracking`, whose `data` is `(n_frames, 3)` = `tongue_x`, `tongue_y`, `tongue_likelihood`, with matching `timestamps`. Column 1 (`tongue_y`) is the value; column 2 (`likelihood`) decides whether the tongue is visible in that frame.\n   396→\n   397→ii.\n   398→```python\n   399→ts = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series['Camera0_side_TongueTracking']\n   400→t_cam = np.asarray(ts.timestamps)\n   401→data = ts.data[:]                        # (n_frames, 3) = x, y, likelihood\n   402→y = data[:, 1].copy()\n   403→```\n   404→\n   405→iii. This is the only tongue measurement in the file. The channel layout is stated in the series' own `description` attribute (`\"('tongue_x', 'tongue_y', 'tongue_likelihood')\"`) rather than assumed. Tracking is present in all 174 sessions and runs at ~294 Hz.\n   406→\n   407→## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *tongue_y_position*?\n   408→\n   409→i. Four steps. Frames with `likelihood < 0.5` are set to NaN, since the tracker still reports a position when the tongue is not protruding. The surviving values are averaged into 50 ms bins across the whole session, and the 40th and 60th percentiles of *those bin means* give two class edges. Each trial's window is then binned the same way and digitised against those edges, giving classes 0/1/2. Bins containing no visible frame are assigned a fourth class, `3` (`'not visible'`).\n   410→\n   411→ii.\n   412→```python\n   413→TONGUE_CONF = 0.5                # tracking likelihood for the tongue to count as visible\n   414→TONGUE_PCT = (40, 60)            # per-session percentiles -> 3 visible classes\n   415→TONGUE_HIDDEN = 3                # no visible tongue frame in the bin\n   416→```\n   417→\n   418→```python\n   419→y[data[:, 2] < TONGUE_CONF] = np.nan     # keep only visible frames\n   420→\n   421→# bin the whole session on one grid, then take percentiles of the bin means\n   422→gidx = np.floor((t_cam - t_cam[0]) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n   423→edges = np.nanpercentile(_bin_mean(gidx, y, int(gidx[-1]) + 1), TONGUE_PCT)\n   424→\n   425→# per trial, bin the same way and digitise against those edges\n   426→cls = np.full((len(go), N_BINS), TONGUE_HIDDEN, np.int8)\n   427→lo = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_START, 'left')\n   428→hi = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_STOP, 'left')\n   429→for i in range(len(go)):\n   430→    b = np.floor((t_cam[lo[i]:hi[i]] - (go[i] + T_START)) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n   431→    np.clip(b, 0, N_BINS - 1, out=b)\n   432→    m = _bin_mean(b, y[lo[i]:hi[i]], N_BINS)\n   433→    ok = ~np.isnan(m)\n   434→    cls[i, ok] = np.digitize(m[ok], edges)\n   435→```\n   436→\n   437→iii. The tongue is visible in only ~10% of frames, and when it is retracted the tracker still emits a position, so those frames are discarded rather than averaged in — including them shifts the percentiles and mixes real protrusions with noise. The likelihood value is effectively binary (89% of frames below 0.01, 10.5% at or above 0.99), so the exact threshold does not matter.\n   438→\n   439→Percentiles are taken over the 50 ms bin means rather than raw frames so the edges are defined on the same quantity that gets discretised; taking them over frames instead skews the resulting class balance, because averaging within a bin pulls values toward the center. The 40th/60th split and the per-session scope both follow the instructions. A fourth class is defined for bins with no visible tongue, which is 75% of all bins.\n   440→\n   441→## 8-d. How is `output` *tongue_y_position* aligned with the neural data?\n   442→\n   443→i. This is the one genuinely time-varying output, so it is the only one needing real alignment. The camera timestamps are on the same session-absolute clock as the spikes and the go cues, so each trial's frame range is found by `searchsorted` on the camera timestamps at `go + T_START` and `go + T_STOP`, and frames are assigned to bins by their offset from `go + T_START` — the same go-cue-relative grid used for the firing rates.\n   444→\n   445→ii.\n   446→```python\n   447→lo = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_START, 'left')\n   448→hi = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_STOP, 'left')\n   449→for i in range(len(go)):\n   450→    b = np.floor((t_cam[lo[i]:hi[i]] - (go[i] + T_START)) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n   451→```\n   452→\n   453→iii. The camera timestamps share the global clock with the spikes and events, so no interpolation or offset correction is needed — the same bin grid is applied to both streams, which guarantees bin `k` of the tongue output covers the same interval as bin `k` of the firing rates.\n   454→\n   455→Unlike the spikes, the video is trial-gated: it runs from `trials.start_time` to the trial-end event and is off during the inter-trial interval. On the ~3% of trials where the go cue falls less than 2.5 s after trial start, the leading bins therefore contain no frames at all and fall into the `'not visible'` class.\n   456→\n   457→## 9. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n   458→\n   459→i. Three cases:\n   460→\n   461→- **Session never quality-controlled**: `classification` and `anno_name` are NaN rather than strings. `_text` maps any non-string entry to `''`, the session then has no `'good'` units, and it is dropped.\n   462→- **Trials without spike data**: excluded via `obs_intervals` and `free_water` (see 1-e).\n   463→- **Frames with no tracked tongue**: set to NaN and excluded from the bin mean; a bin left with no valid frame becomes the `'not visible'` class.\n   464→\n   465→ii.\n   466→```python\n   467→def _text(col):\n   468→    \"\"\"Read a text column; non-str entries (unlabelled sessions) become ''.\"\"\"\n   469→    return np.array([x if isinstance(x, str) else '' for x in col[:]])\n   470→```\n   471→\n   472→```python\n   473→good = np.flatnonzero(cls == 'good')\n   474→if good.size == 0:\n   475→    return None\n   476→```\n   477→\n   478→```python\n   479→y[data[:, 2] < TONGUE_CONF] = np.nan     # keep only visible frames\n   480→...\n   481→m = _bin_mean(b, y[lo[i]:hi[i]], N_BINS)\n   482→ok = ~np.isnan(m)\n   483→cls[i, ok] = np.digitize(m[ok], edges)\n   484→```\n   485→\n   486→iii. Missing data is handled one of two ways depending on what it means. Where nothing was recorded, the session or trial is excluded, since emitting it would fabricate data — a trial with no spikes would otherwise appear as 4 s of 0 Hz across every unit. Where the measurement legitimately has no value, as with a retracted tongue, it is represented as an explicit category rather than imputed.\n   487→\n   488→## 10-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n   489→\n   490→i. Reading each NWB file dominates. The full conversion takes 247 s for 174 sessions, ~1.4 s per session on average, ranging from ~0.7 s to ~4 s roughly with unit count. Within a session the costs are pulling the `spike_times` buffer (up to ~11.5 M doubles) and the tongue tracking array (~680 k x 3), then the per-unit `searchsorted` loop. Pickling the 11.9 GB result takes a further ~40 s.\n   491→\n   492→ii. N/A\n   493→\n   494→iii. The work is dominated by I/O and by one binary search per bin edge per unit, both of which scale with the data actually needed. No step was worth optimising further given the 15-minute budget in the instructions.\n   495→\n   496→## 10-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n   497→\n   498→i. Two loops remain. The per-unit loop in the neural binning runs one `searchsorted` per unit, but over *all* trials at once — the per-trial dimension is already vectorised by flattening the edge array. The per-trial loop in `tongue_output` bins one trial's frames at a time.\n   499→\n   500→ii.\n   501→```python\n   502→edges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n   503→for r, u in enumerate(good):\n   504→    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n   505→    pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n   506→```\n   507→\n   508→iii. The per-unit loop cannot be collapsed further because each unit has a different number of spikes, so there is no single sorted array to search — this is inherent to the ragged storage. The tongue loop could be vectorised with a global bin index and one `bincount`, but it runs over trials rather than frames and is not a measurable share of runtime, so it was left in the clearer form.\n   509→\n   510→## 10-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n   511→\n   512→i. Nothing is recomputed. Each NWB file is opened once and every quantity derived from it is computed once. The bin grid (`REL_EDGES`, `CENTERS`) is built once at module level and reused for every trial and session.\n   513→\n   514→ii. N/A\n   515→\n   516→iii. The conversion is a single pass over the files. Because the tongue discretisation edges are per-session rather than global, they can be computed inside that same pass — no second pass over the data is needed to establish them.\n   517→\n   518→## 10-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n   519→\n   520→i. None. Every field computed goes into the output.\n   521→\n   522→ii. N/A\n   523→\n   524→iii. N/A\n   525→\n   526→## 10-e. How is memory usage optimized?\n   527→\n   528→i. Firing rates are stored as `float32` and the outputs as `int8` rather than the default `float64`. Each session is read inside a `with` block so the file handle and its cached arrays are released on exit, and only the per-trial slices are retained. The full neural payload is 11.9 GB; a single session's working arrays are at most ~0.2 GB.\n   529→\n   530→ii.\n   531→```python\n   532→rates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\n   533→```\n   534→\n   535→```python\n   536→out = np.empty((n_trials, len(OUTPUT_NAMES), N_BINS), np.int8)\n   537→```\n   538→\n   539→```python\n   540→inp = np.stack([time_from_tone, photostim], axis=1).astype(np.float32)\n   541→```\n   542→\n   543→iii. `float32` halves the largest object in the output relative to `float64` at no cost, since firing rates are multiples of 20 Hz and well within its precision. Outputs are small non-negative integers, so `int8` is sufficient. Peak memory is set by the accumulated result rather than by any single session, so no further streaming was needed.\n   544→"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"e210ca83-4693-49e1-8fa6-935ef7923b34","uuid":"3ce214e7-19c5-4b5d-9874-cba3fcfb3afb","timestamp":"2026-09-23T18:48:54.768Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/tests/reference_DECISIONS.md","content":"# Decisions\n\n## 1-a. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. The dataset is distributed as one NWB file per session under `data/sub-<subject_id>/`. All sessions are found with a single glob over that layout, and each file is opened with `pynwb` and processed once. Subjects, trials, and units are then read from within each file (`nwb.subject`, `nwb.trials`, `nwb.units`, `nwb.acquisition`).\n\nii. Finding all data:\n```python\nfiles = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, 'sub-*', '*.nwb')))\n...\nfor i, path in enumerate(files, 1):\n    res = process_session(path)\n```\n\nLoading one session:\n```python\nwith NWBHDF5IO(path, mode='r', load_namespaces=True) as io:\n    nwb = io.read()\n    units = nwb.units\n    trials = nwb.trials.to_dataframe()\n    bev = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralEvents'].time_series\n```\n\niii. NWB is the published format for this dataset and `pynwb` is its standard reader. Since there is one file per session, the directory listing is the complete set of sessions and a glob is sufficient; sorting it makes the session order deterministic. The resulting counts (174 files, 28 subjects) match `assetsSummary` in `data/dandiset.yaml`.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n\ni. Each NWB file records its animal in `nwb.subject.subject_id`, a numeric string such as `'440956'`. That value is read for every session and carried through to assembly, where `subjects` is the sorted set of unique ids and `subject_idx` gives each session's index into that list.\n\nii. Per session:\n```python\nreturn {\n    'session_id': nwb.identifier,\n    'subject': nwb.subject.subject_id,\n    ...\n```\n\nAt assembly:\n```python\nsubjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\nsub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n...\n'subjects': subjects,\n'subject_idx': np.array([sub_ix[r['subject']] for r in results], np.int32),\n```\n\niii. `subject_id` is the canonical animal identifier in the file, and the containing folder name (`sub-440956`) is derived from it, so no separate grouping step is needed. This gives 28 subjects with 3-10 sessions each, matching the dandiset. The numeric id differs from the mouse name used in the papers — `nwb.identifier` is e.g. `SC015_20190207_120657_s1`, so subject `440956` is mouse `SC015` — but the numeric id is what the NWB subject field provides, so it is used directly.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. One NWB file is one session, so no grouping or splitting is needed. Each session is identified by `nwb.identifier` (e.g. `SC015_20190207_120657_s1`, encoding mouse, date, time and session number), recorded per session and also listed in `metadata['session_info']`. Session order in the output follows the sorted file list.\n\nii.\n```python\nfiles = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, 'sub-*', '*.nwb')))\n```\n\n```python\n'session_id': nwb.identifier,\n```\n\n```python\n'session_info': [\n    {'session_id': r['session_id'], 'subject': r['subject'],\n     'n_trials': r['n_trials'], 'n_units': r['n_units']}\n    for r in results\n],\n```\n\niii. The dandiset already stores one session per file, so the file boundary is the session boundary and nothing has to be inferred. Because the filename embeds the acquisition timestamp (`sub-440956_ses-20190207T120657_...`), sorting the paths puts sessions in chronological order within each subject. 173 of the 174 files reach the output; the one exception is dropped for having no quality-controlled units (see 2-c).\n\n## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n\ni. Trials come from the NWB trials table (`nwb.trials`), one row per behavioural trial, with exactly one go-cue event per row. The row count is checked against the number of go-cue events.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrials = nwb.trials.to_dataframe()\ngo = np.asarray(bev['go_start_times'].timestamps)\nassert len(go) == len(trials), f'{len(go)} go cues vs {len(trials)} trials'\n```\n\niii. Trials are clearly defined by the trials table, so it is used directly rather than re-deriving boundaries from the event streams. `go_start_times` has exactly one event per trial in all 174 sessions, which makes the mapping unambiguous. This is not true of the other trial-phase events: `sample_start_times` and `delay_start_times` can have several entries per trial, because a lick during the sample or delay replays that epoch.\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Trials are filtered only for absence of spike data, in two cases. First, trials outside `units/obs_intervals`: in 8 sessions the ephys recording starts after the behaviour, leaving up to 376 leading trials with no spikes. Second, `free_water` trials, which have no spikes anywhere in the window even though `obs_intervals` covers them. A session is dropped entirely if fewer than 2 trials survive. No behavioural quality filter is applied.\n\nii.\n```python\n# filter behavioral trials with no spike data\noi_off = np.asarray(units['obs_intervals'].data)\noi_start = np.concatenate([[0], oi_off[:-1]])\nobs = np.asarray(units['obs_intervals'].target.data)[oi_start[good[0]]:oi_off[good[0]]]\nkeep = np.isin(np.round(trials['start_time'].values, 4), np.round(obs[:, 0], 4))\nassert keep.sum() == len(obs), f'{keep.sum()} matched vs {len(obs)} observed'\n\n# filter no water trials\nkeep &= trials['free_water'].values == 0\nif keep.sum() < 2:\n    return None\ntrials = trials[keep]\ngo = go[keep]\n```\n\niii. `obs_intervals` is the file's own record of which trials were observed, and its start times match `trials.start_time`, which makes the mapping exact. The `free_water` exclusion was found empirically — those trials are covered by `obs_intervals` yet contain no spikes at all — and accounts for 2,449 of the 2,451 all-zero trials remaining after the `obs_intervals` filter.\n\nEarly-lick and no-response (`ignore`) trials are deliberately kept, even though the data paper states \"Early lick trials and no response trials were excluded for analysis\", because both are required decoder outputs here. The 2-trial minimum is the target format's requirement.\n\nTogether these remove 3,510 of the 94,370 trials in the retained sessions (3.7%), leaving 90,860.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from `units/spike_times`, the sorted spike times of each unit in session-absolute seconds. Only units with `classification == 'good'` contribute (see 2-c). The go-cue times (`BehavioralEvents/go_start_times`) are the other input, used to place the bin edges.\n\nii.\n```python\noffs = np.asarray(units['spike_times'].data)\nallst = np.asarray(units['spike_times'].target.data)\nstarts = np.concatenate([[0], offs[:-1]])\n\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\nrates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\nfor r, u in enumerate(good):\n    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n```\n\niii. `spike_times` is the only neural representation in the file, so firing rates are computed from it directly.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Spike times are converted to per-bin firing rates in Hz. For each good unit, the bin edges for every trial are built as one flat array of absolute times, `np.searchsorted` gives the running spike count at each edge, and differencing adjacent counts gives the spike count per bin. Counts are divided by the bin width to give Hz. No smoothing, normalisation, or baseline subtraction is applied.\n\nii.\n```python\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\nrates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\nfor r, u in enumerate(good):\n    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n    # running spike total at each edge; differencing gives the count per bin\n    pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n    rates[r] = np.diff(pos, axis=1)\nrates /= BIN                                  # counts -> Hz\n```\n\niii. Firing rate is computed from the binned spike count over the 50 ms window defined in the instructions. This also matches the reference code, which calls `sliding_histogram(..., rate=True)` and returns `binSpikes / bin_width`.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Only units with `units/classification == 'good'` are kept. No thresholds are applied to any individual quality metric. A session with no such units is dropped entirely. This retains 69,453 of 272,227 units (25.5%), a median of 390 per session.\n\nii.\n```python\n# ---- units: QC classifier verdict ----------------------------------\ncls = _text(units['classification'])\ngood = np.flatnonzero(cls == 'good')\nif good.size == 0:\n    return None\n```\n\niii. `classification` is the verdict of the spike-sorting quality-control classifier described in `ChenLiuEtAl2023_SpikeSortingQC.pdf`.\n\n`units/unit_quality` ('good' / 'multi') is deliberately not used: it is an older label that disagrees on 12.2% of the classifier-good units and is far more permissive.\n\nThe one dropped session (`sub-440958_ses-20190216T162508`) has `classification` and `anno_name` set to NaN for all 1,852 units, i.e. it was never quality-controlled. Excluding it gives 173 sessions and 69,453 good units, against the white paper's 173 sessions and 69,943 units.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Spike times and event times are already on the same session-absolute clock, so no alignment step is needed. The bin edges relative to the go cue are added to each trial's go-cue time to give the absolute time window for that trial, and the spikes are binned against those edges directly.\n\nii.\n```python\ngo = np.asarray(bev['go_start_times'].timestamps)\n```\n\n```python\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\nrates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\nfor r, u in enumerate(good):\n    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n    pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n    rates[r] = np.diff(pos, axis=1)\n```\n\niii. Everything in the NWB file is timestamped on one global clock, so aligning to the go cue only requires looking up each trial's go-cue time and taking the window around it. There is no resampling or interpolation, and no per-stream offset to correct.\n\n## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n\ni. Spike times are binned into 80 non-overlapping 50 ms bins spanning -2.5 s to +1.5 s relative to the go cue. The bin grid is defined once, as 81 edges relative to the go cue, and reused for every trial and session, so every trial has the same 80 timepoints.\n\nii.\n```python\n# trial window relative to the go cue, and bin width, in seconds\nT_START, T_STOP = -2.5, 1.5\nBIN = 0.05\nN_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))             \n\nREL_EDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)         \nCENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        \n```\n\n```python\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n```\n\niii. The window and the 50 ms bin width are set by the instructions. Defining the grid once as offsets from the go cue gives a constant 80 timepoints per trial, which the target format requires.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_from_tone_onset* derived from?\n\ni. From `sample_start_times`, the tone onsets of the session, together with the go cue of each trial. The tone taken for a trial is the **last one** before its go cue.\n\nii. The tone of each trial:\n```python\nsample = np.asarray(bev['sample_start_times'].timestamps)\ntone = sample[np.searchsorted(sample, go, side='left') - 1]\n```\n\niii. An early lick replays the sample epoch, so a trial can carry more than one tone; the last one before the go cue is the one the animal actually used.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_from_tone_onset*?\n\ni. The bins sit around the go cue, so the value of a bin is its center plus the gap between the tone and the go cue.\n\nii. The bin center, and the shift that turns them into time from the tone:\n```python\nCENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        \n```\n```python\n# bins sit around the go cue, values are seconds since the tone\ntime_from_tone = CENTERS[None, :] + (go - tone)[:, None]\n```\n\niii. No additional processing needed other than find the tone onset time.\n\n## 3-c. How is `input` *time_from_tone_onset* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. It is how the neural binnning grid is defined.\n\nii. The grid, defined once as offsets from the go cue, and laid on each trial's go cue to bin the spikes:\n```python\nT_START, T_STOP = -2.5, 1.5\nBIN = 0.05\nN_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))             \n\nREL_EDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)         \nCENTERS = REL_EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                        \n```\n```python\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n```\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *photostim* derived from?\n\ni. From `photostim_onset` and `photostim_duration` in the trials table, with `start_time` and the go cue used to place them on the trial's time axis.\n\nii. The onset and offset of the stimulation, relative to the go cue:\n```python\non_s = trials['photostim_onset'].values\nhas = on_s != 'N/A'\nstim_on[has] = trials['start_time'].values[has] + on_s[has].astype(float) - go[has]\nstim_off[has] = stim_on[has] + trials['photostim_duration'].values[has].astype(float)\n```\n\niii. The onsets are stored as strings measured from trial start, with `'N/A'` on the trials that were not stimulated, so they have to be converted and re-expressed against the go cue the bins are aligned on.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *photostim*?\n\ni. A bin is 1 where its center falls between the onset and the offset of the stimulation and 0 elsewhere, so the input is a binary time series rather than a per trial flag.\n\nii. The bins the light is on for:\n```python\n# NaN compares False, so non-stim trials stay 0\nphotostim = ((CENTERS[None, :] >= stim_on[:, None])\n             & (CENTERS[None, :] < stim_off[:, None]))\n```\n\niii. A trial without stimulation keeps NaN bounds, and NaN compares false, so all of its bins come out 0 without a separate branch.\n\n## 4-c. How is `input` *photostim* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The onset and offset are expressed relative to the go cue, which is the event the neural bins are aligned on, so they can be compared against the bin center directly.\n\nii. The onset put on the same axis as the bins:\n```python\nstim_on[has] = trials['start_time'].values[has] + on_s[has].astype(float) - go[has]\n```\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *choice* derived from?\n\ni. There is no choice column in the file. Choice is derived from two trials-table columns: `trial_instruction` (`'left'` / `'right'`, the side the tone instructed) and `outcome` (`'hit'` / `'miss'` / `'ignore'`). A hit means the animal licked the instructed side, a miss means it licked the other side, and an `ignore` means it never licked.\n\nii.\n```python\nSIDE_CODE = {'left': 0, 'right': 1}\nCHOICE_NO_LICK = 2               # no lick in the response window\n```\n\n```python\n# choice is not stored; derive it from instruction x outcome\noutcome_s = trials['outcome'].values\nside = np.array([SIDE_CODE[x] for x in trials['trial_instruction'].values])\nchoice = np.where(outcome_s == 'ignore', CHOICE_NO_LICK,\n                  np.where(outcome_s == 'hit', side, 1 - side))\n```\n\niii. The animal's actual lick direction is not stored, but it is fully determined by the instructed side and the outcome, so it is derived from those two columns. `outcome == 'ignore'` was verified to mean no lick anywhere in `[go, go + 1.5]`, with no exceptions, so choice is genuinely undefined on those trials and gets its own third value.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *choice*?\n\ni. The derived choice is coded as `0` left, `1` right, `2` no lick, and written into row 0 of the per-trial output array, repeated across all 80 bins. `output_values[0]` names the three codes.\n\nii.\n```python\nSIDE_CODE = {'left': 0, 'right': 1}\nCHOICE_NO_LICK = 2               # no lick in the response window\n\nOUTPUT_VALUES = [\n    ['left', 'right', 'no lick'],\n    ...\n]\n```\n\n```python\n# choice is not stored; derive it from instruction x outcome\noutcome_s = trials['outcome'].values\nside = np.array([SIDE_CODE[x] for x in trials['trial_instruction'].values])\nchoice = np.where(outcome_s == 'ignore', CHOICE_NO_LICK,\n                  np.where(outcome_s == 'hit', side, 1 - side))\n\n# per-trial values are repeated across bins so all outputs share one array\nout = np.empty((n_trials, len(OUTPUT_NAMES), N_BINS), np.int8)\nout[:, 0, :] = choice[:, None]\n```\n\niii. `left = 0` and `right = 1` follow the instructions, and a third class is defined for the no-lick case. Choice is one value per trial, so it is repeated across the 80 bins to keep all four outputs in a single `(n_output, n_timepoints)` array.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Outcome comes directly from the `outcome` column of the trials table, which already holds the strings `'ignore'`, `'miss'`, and `'hit'`.\n\nii.\n```python\noutcome_s = trials['outcome'].values\n```\n\niii. The trials table stores the outcome explicitly with exactly the three categories the instructions ask for, so no derivation is needed.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *outcome*?\n\ni. The three strings are mapped to `0` ignore, `1` miss, `2` hit via a fixed dictionary, and written into row 1 of the output array, repeated across all 80 bins.\n\nii.\n```python\nOUTCOME_CODE = {'ignore': 0, 'miss': 1, 'hit': 2}\n```\n\n```python\nout[:, 1, :] = np.array([OUTCOME_CODE[x] for x in outcome_s])[:, None]\n```\n\niii. The code assignment follows the instructions. Outcome is one value per trial, so it is repeated across bins like the other per-trial outputs.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *early_lick* derived from?\n\ni. From the `early_lick` column of the trials table, which holds the strings `'no early'` and `'early'`.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrials['early_lick'].values\n```\n\niii. The trials table flags early licking explicitly, so no derivation is needed. The lick that sets the flag occurs during the sample or delay epoch, before the go cue, so the event itself falls inside the -2.5 s window even though the flag is stored per trial.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *early_lick*?\n\ni. The two strings are mapped to `0` no, `1` yes via a fixed dictionary, and written into row 2 of the output array, repeated across all 80 bins.\n\nii.\n```python\nEARLY_CODE = {'no early': 0, 'early': 1}\n```\n\n```python\nout[:, 2, :] = np.array([EARLY_CODE[x] for x in trials['early_lick'].values])[:, None]\n```\n\niii. The code assignment follows the instructions. One value per trial, so repeated across bins like the other per-trial outputs.\n\n## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *tongue_y_position* derived from?\n\ni. From `acquisition/BehavioralTimeSeries/Camera0_side_TongueTracking`, whose `data` is `(n_frames, 3)` = `tongue_x`, `tongue_y`, `tongue_likelihood`, with matching `timestamps`. Column 1 (`tongue_y`) is the value; column 2 (`likelihood`) decides whether the tongue is visible in that frame.\n\nii.\n```python\nts = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series['Camera0_side_TongueTracking']\nt_cam = np.asarray(ts.timestamps)\ndata = ts.data[:]                        # (n_frames, 3) = x, y, likelihood\ny = data[:, 1].copy()\n```\n\niii. This is the only tongue measurement in the file. The channel layout is stated in the series' own `description` attribute (`\"('tongue_x', 'tongue_y', 'tongue_likelihood')\"`) rather than assumed. Tracking is present in all 174 sessions and runs at ~294 Hz.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *tongue_y_position*?\n\ni. Four steps. Frames with `likelihood < 0.5` are set to NaN, since the tracker still reports a position when the tongue is not protruding. The surviving values are averaged into 50 ms bins across the whole session, and the 40th and 60th percentiles of *those bin means* give two class edges. Each trial's window is then binned the same way and digitised against those edges, giving classes 0/1/2. Bins containing no visible frame are assigned a fourth class, `3` (`'not visible'`).\n\nii.\n```python\nTONGUE_CONF = 0.5                # tracking likelihood for the tongue to count as visible\nTONGUE_PCT = (40, 60)            # per-session percentiles -> 3 visible classes\nTONGUE_HIDDEN = 3                # no visible tongue frame in the bin\n```\n\n```python\ny[data[:, 2] < TONGUE_CONF] = np.nan     # keep only visible frames\n\n# bin the whole session on one grid, then take percentiles of the bin means\ngidx = np.floor((t_cam - t_cam[0]) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\nedges = np.nanpercentile(_bin_mean(gidx, y, int(gidx[-1]) + 1), TONGUE_PCT)\n\n# per trial, bin the same way and digitise against those edges\ncls = np.full((len(go), N_BINS), TONGUE_HIDDEN, np.int8)\nlo = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_START, 'left')\nhi = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_STOP, 'left')\nfor i in range(len(go)):\n    b = np.floor((t_cam[lo[i]:hi[i]] - (go[i] + T_START)) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n    np.clip(b, 0, N_BINS - 1, out=b)\n    m = _bin_mean(b, y[lo[i]:hi[i]], N_BINS)\n    ok = ~np.isnan(m)\n    cls[i, ok] = np.digitize(m[ok], edges)\n```\n\niii. The tongue is visible in only ~10% of frames, and when it is retracted the tracker still emits a position, so those frames are discarded rather than averaged in — including them shifts the percentiles and mixes real protrusions with noise. The likelihood value is effectively binary (89% of frames below 0.01, 10.5% at or above 0.99), so the exact threshold does not matter.\n\nPercentiles are taken over the 50 ms bin means rather than raw frames so the edges are defined on the same quantity that gets discretised; taking them over frames instead skews the resulting class balance, because averaging within a bin pulls values toward the center. The 40th/60th split and the per-session scope both follow the instructions. A fourth class is defined for bins with no visible tongue, which is 75% of all bins.\n\n## 8-d. How is `output` *tongue_y_position* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. This is the one genuinely time-varying output, so it is the only one needing real alignment. The camera timestamps are on the same session-absolute clock as the spikes and the go cues, so each trial's frame range is found by `searchsorted` on the camera timestamps at `go + T_START` and `go + T_STOP`, and frames are assigned to bins by their offset from `go + T_START` — the same go-cue-relative grid used for the firing rates.\n\nii.\n```python\nlo = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_START, 'left')\nhi = np.searchsorted(t_cam, go + T_STOP, 'left')\nfor i in range(len(go)):\n    b = np.floor((t_cam[lo[i]:hi[i]] - (go[i] + T_START)) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. The camera timestamps share the global clock with the spikes and events, so no interpolation or offset correction is needed — the same bin grid is applied to both streams, which guarantees bin `k` of the tongue output covers the same interval as bin `k` of the firing rates.\n\nUnlike the spikes, the video is trial-gated: it runs from `trials.start_time` to the trial-end event and is off during the inter-trial interval. On the ~3% of trials where the go cue falls less than 2.5 s after trial start, the leading bins therefore contain no frames at all and fall into the `'not visible'` class.\n\n## 9. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Three cases:\n\n- **Session never quality-controlled**: `classification` and `anno_name` are NaN rather than strings. `_text` maps any non-string entry to `''`, the session then has no `'good'` units, and it is dropped.\n- **Trials without spike data**: excluded via `obs_intervals` and `free_water` (see 1-e).\n- **Frames with no tracked tongue**: set to NaN and excluded from the bin mean; a bin left with no valid frame becomes the `'not visible'` class.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef _text(col):\n    \"\"\"Read a text column; non-str entries (unlabelled sessions) become ''.\"\"\"\n    return np.array([x if isinstance(x, str) else '' for x in col[:]])\n```\n\n```python\ngood = np.flatnonzero(cls == 'good')\nif good.size == 0:\n    return None\n```\n\n```python\ny[data[:, 2] < TONGUE_CONF] = np.nan     # keep only visible frames\n...\nm = _bin_mean(b, y[lo[i]:hi[i]], N_BINS)\nok = ~np.isnan(m)\ncls[i, ok] = np.digitize(m[ok], edges)\n```\n\niii. Missing data is handled one of two ways depending on what it means. Where nothing was recorded, the session or trial is excluded, since emitting it would fabricate data — a trial with no spikes would otherwise appear as 4 s of 0 Hz across every unit. Where the measurement legitimately has no value, as with a retracted tongue, it is represented as an explicit category rather than imputed.\n\n## 10-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. Reading each NWB file dominates. The full conversion takes 247 s for 174 sessions, ~1.4 s per session on average, ranging from ~0.7 s to ~4 s roughly with unit count. Within a session the costs are pulling the `spike_times` buffer (up to ~11.5 M doubles) and the tongue tracking array (~680 k x 3), then the per-unit `searchsorted` loop. Pickling the 11.9 GB result takes a further ~40 s.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The work is dominated by I/O and by one binary search per bin edge per unit, both of which scale with the data actually needed. No step was worth optimising further given the 15-minute budget in the instructions.\n\n## 10-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. Two loops remain. The per-unit loop in the neural binning runs one `searchsorted` per unit, but over *all* trials at once — the per-trial dimension is already vectorised by flattening the edge array. The per-trial loop in `tongue_output` bins one trial's frames at a time.\n\nii.\n```python\nedges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\nfor r, u in enumerate(good):\n    s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n    pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n```\n\niii. The per-unit loop cannot be collapsed further because each unit has a different number of spikes, so there is no single sorted array to search — this is inherent to the ragged storage. The tongue loop could be vectorised with a global bin index and one `bincount`, but it runs over trials rather than frames and is not a measurable share of runtime, so it was left in the clearer form.\n\n## 10-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. Nothing is recomputed. Each NWB file is opened once and every quantity derived from it is computed once. The bin grid (`REL_EDGES`, `CENTERS`) is built once at module level and reused for every trial and session.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The conversion is a single pass over the files. Because the tongue discretisation edges are per-session rather than global, they can be computed inside that same pass — no second pass over the data is needed to establish them.\n\n## 10-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. None. Every field computed goes into the output.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 10-e. How is memory usage optimized?\n\ni. Firing rates are stored as `float32` and the outputs as `int8` rather than the default `float64`. Each session is read inside a `with` block so the file handle and its cached arrays are released on exit, and only the per-trial slices are retained. The full neural payload is 11.9 GB; a single session's working arrays are at most ~0.2 GB.\n\nii.\n```python\nrates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\n```\n\n```python\nout = np.empty((n_trials, len(OUTPUT_NAMES), N_BINS), np.int8)\n```\n\n```python\ninp = np.stack([time_from_tone, photostim], axis=1).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. `float32` halves the largest object in the output relative to `float64` at no cost, since firing rates are multiples of 20 Hz and well within its precision. Outputs are small non-negative integers, so `int8` is sufficient. Peak memory is set by the accumulated result rather than by any single session, so no further streaming was needed.\n","numLines":544,"startLine":1,"totalLines":544}}}

Returns a dict, or None if the session is dropped.\"\"\"\n    with NWBHDF5IO(path, mode='r', load_namespaces=True) as io:\n        nwb = io.read()\n        units = nwb.units\n        trials = nwb.trials.to_dataframe()\n        bev = nwb.acquisition['BehavioralEvents'].time_series\n\n        # ---- units: QC classifier verdict ----------------------------------\n        cls = _text(units['classification'])\n        good = np.flatnonzero(cls == 'good')\n        if good.size == 0:\n            return None\n        regions = [region_label(a) for a in _text(units['anno_name'])[good]]\n\n        # all NWB times are session-absolute seconds\n        go = np.asarray(bev['go_start_times'].timestamps)\n        assert len(go) == len(trials), f'{len(go)} go cues vs {len(trials)} trials'\n\n        # ---- trials filtering ----\n        # spike data\n        # obs_intervals gives one [start, stop] per observed trial, which in some\n        # sessions is fewer than the behavioural trials. \n        # a behavioural trial is observed if its start time is one of the interval starts\n        obs = np.asarray(units['obs_intervals'][good[0]])        \n        keep = np.isin(trials['start_time'].values, obs[:, 0])\n        assert keep.sum() == len(obs), f'{keep.sum()} matched vs {len(obs)} observed'\n\n        # filter no water trials\n        keep &= trials['free_water'].values == 0\n        \n        # a sessioon has to have at least 2 trials according to the instruction\n        if keep.sum() < 2:\n            return None\n        \n        trials = trials[keep]\n        go = go[keep]\n        n_trials = len(go)\n\n        # last tone onset before the go cue; an early lick replays the sample epoch\n        sample = np.asarray(bev['sample_start_times'].timestamps)\n        tone = sample[np.searchsorted(sample, go, side='left') - 1]\n\n        # ---- inputs --------------------------------------------------------\n        # bins sit around the go cue, values are seconds since the tone\n        time_from_tone = CENTERS[None, :] + (go - tone)[:, None]\n\n        # photostim onsets are stored as strings relative to trial start\n        on_s = trials['photostim_onset'].values\n        has = on_s != 'N/A'\n        stim_on = np.full(n_trials, np.nan)\n        stim_on[has] = trials['start_time'].values[has] + on_s[has].astype(float) - go[has]\n        stim_off = np.full(n_trials, np.nan)\n        stim_off[has] = stim_on[has] + trials['photostim_duration'].values[has].astype(float)\n        # NaN compares False, so non-stim trials stay 0\n        photostim = ((CENTERS[None, :] >= stim_on[:, None])\n                     & (CENTERS[None, :] < stim_off[:, None]))\n\n        inp = np.stack([time_from_tone, photostim], axis=1).astype(np.float32)\n\n        # ---- outputs -------------------------------------------------------\n        # choice is not stored; derive it from instruction x outcome\n        outcome_s = trials['outcome'].values\n        side = np.array([SIDE_CODE[x] for x in trials['trial_instruction'].values])\n        choice = np.where(outcome_s == 'ignore', CHOICE_NO_LICK,\n                          np.where(outcome_s == 'hit', side, 1 - side))\n\n        # per-trial values are repeated across bins so all outputs share one array\n        out = np.empty((n_trials, len(OUTPUT_NAMES), N_BINS), np.int8)\n        out[:, 0, :] = choice[:, None]\n        out[:, 1, :] = np.array([OUTCOME_CODE[x] for x in outcome_s])[:, None]\n        out[:, 2, :] = np.array([EARLY_CODE[x] for x in trials['early_lick'].values])[:, None]\n        out[:, 3, :] = tongue_output(nwb, go)\n\n        # ---- neural --------------------------------------------------------\n        # spike_times is ragged: read the buffer once instead of one read per unit\n        offs = np.asarray(units['spike_times'].data)\n        allst = np.asarray(units['spike_times'].target.data)\n        starts = np.concatenate([[0], offs[:-1]])\n\n        edges = (go[:, None] + REL_EDGES[None, :]).ravel()\n        rates = np.empty((good.size, n_trials, N_BINS), np.float32)\n        for r, u in enumerate(good):\n            s = allst[starts[u]:offs[u]]\n            # running spike total at each edge; differencing gives the count per bin\n            pos = np.searchsorted(s, edges).reshape(n_trials, N_BINS + 1)\n            rates[r] = np.diff(pos, axis=1)\n        rates /= BIN                                  # counts -> Hz\n\n        return {\n            'session_id': nwb.identifier,\n            'subject': nwb.subject.subject_id,\n            'neural': [np.ascontiguousarray(rates[:, t, :]) for t in range(n_trials)],\n            'input': [inp[t] for t in range(n_trials)],\n            'output': [out[t] for t in range(n_trials)],\n            'regions': regions,\n            'n_trials': n_trials,\n            'n_units': int(good.size),\n        }\n\n\ndef assemble(results):\n    \"\"\"Stitch per-session results into the target dictionary.\"\"\"\n    subjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\n    sub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n\n    brain_regions = sorted({g for r in results for g in r['regions']})\n    reg_ix = {g: i for i, g in enumerate(brain_regions)}\n\n    return {\n        'neural': [r['neural'] for r in results],\n        'input': [r['input'] for r in results],\n        'output': [r['output'] for r in results],\n\n        'subjects': subjects,\n        'subject_idx': np.array([sub_ix[r['subject']] for r in results], np.int32),\n\n        'brain_regions': brain_regions,\n        'brain_region_idx': [np.array([reg_ix[g] for g in r['regions']], np.int32)\n                             for r in results],\n\n        'input_names': INPUT_NAMES,\n        'output_names': OUTPUT_NAMES,\n        'output_values': OUTPUT_VALUES,\n\n        'metadata': {\n            'task_description':\n                'Auditory delayed-response task. A 3 kHz or 12 kHz tone (0.65 s '\n                'sample epoch) instructs a left or right lick, followed by a 1.2 s '\n                'delay; a 6 kHz go cue opens a 1.5 s response window. Licking '\n                'during sample or delay replays that epoch. Decoded variables: '\n                'lick direction, outcome, early lick, tongue y-position.',\n            'time_bin_size': BIN * 1000.0,          # ms\n            'temporal_alignment_event': 'go cue onset (auditory, 6 kHz, 0.1 s)',\n            'off_start': T_START,\n            'off_end': T_STOP,\n\n            'dataset': 'DANDI 000363, Mesoscale Activity Map (Chen et al. 2023)',\n            'neural_units': 'Hz',\n            'unit_curation':\n                \"units/classification == 'good', the QC classifier of Chen, \"\n                'Colonell, Li & Svoboda (2023). No metric thresholds applied.',\n            'trial_curation':\n                'Trials without spike data excluded: outside units/obs_intervals, '\n                'or free_water.',\n            'tongue_discretisation':\n                'Side-camera tongue y, likelihood < %.2f discarded, survivors '\n                'averaged into %d ms bins; class edges are the %dth/%dth percentiles '\n                'of the session bin means. Bins with no visible frame get the '\n                'trailing \"not visible\" class.'\n                % (TONGUE_CONF, BIN * 1000, TONGUE_PCT[0], TONGUE_PCT[1]),\n            'session_info': [\n                {'session_id': r['session_id'], 'subject': r['subject'],\n                 'n_trials': r['n_trials'], 'n_units': r['n_units']}\n                for r in results\n            ],\n        },\n    }\n\n\ndef main():\n    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=__doc__)\n    ap.add_argument('outfile', help='output pickle path')\n    ap.add_argument('--full', action='store_true', help='process all sessions (default)')\n    ap.add_argument('--sample', action='store_true', help='process only 2 sessions')\n    ap.add_argument('--datadir', default=DATA_DIR,\n                    help='directory holding the sub-*/ NWB files (default: %(default)s)')\n    args = ap.parse_args()\n\n    files = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(args.datadir, 'sub-*', '*.nwb')))\n    if not files:\n        sys.exit('no NWB files under %s' % args.datadir)\n    if args.sample:\n        files = files[:2]\n    print('%d session files' % len(files), flush=True)\n\n    t0 = time.time()\n    results = []\n    dropped = []\n    for i, path in enumerate(files, 1):\n        t1 = time.time()\n        res = process_session(path)\n        if res is None:\n            dropped.append(os.path.basename(path))\n            print('[%3d/%d] dropped %s' % (i, len(files), os.path.basename(path)), flush=True)\n            continue\n        results.append(res)\n        elapsed = time.time() - t0\n        print('[%3d/%d] %-26s %4d trials %4d units  %5.1fs  (elapsed %5.1fs, total ~%5.1fs)'\n              % (i, len(files), res['session_id'], res['n_trials'], res['n_units'],\n                 time.time() - t1, elapsed, elapsed / i * len(files)), flush=True)\n\n    if not results:\n        sys.exit('no sessions survived conversion')\n    print('\\nconverted in %.1f s;
